<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:172px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Faroe_stamp_048_europe_(v_u_hammershaimb).jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/29/Faroe_stamp_048_europe_%28v_u_hammershaimb%29.jpg/170px-Faroe_stamp_048_europe_%28v_u_hammershaimb%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="228" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/29/Faroe_stamp_048_europe_%28v_u_hammershaimb%29.jpg/255px-Faroe_stamp_048_europe_%28v_u_hammershaimb%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/2/29/Faroe_stamp_048_europe_%28v_u_hammershaimb%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="265" data-file-height="355" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Faroe_stamp_048_europe_(v_u_hammershaimb).jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>A stamp commemorating <a href="/wiki/V._U._Hammershaimb" class="mw-redirect" title="V. U. Hammershaimb">V. U. Hammershaimb</a>, a 19th-century Faroese linguist and theologian</div></div></div>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Language">Language</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Faroe_Islands&amp;action=edit&amp;section=14" title="Edit section: Language">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Faroese_language" title="Faroese language">Faroese language</a></div>
<p>As stipulated in section 11 (§ 11) in the 1948 Home Rule Act,<sup id="cite_ref-:0_75-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:0-75">&#91;74&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-76" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-76">&#91;75&#93;</a></sup> Faroese is the primary and official language of the country, whereas Danish is taught in schools and can be used by the Faroese government in public relations, with public services providing Danish translations of documents on request.<sup id="cite_ref-:0_75-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:0-75">&#91;74&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-folang_77-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-folang-77">&#91;76&#93;</a></sup> Faroese belongs to the <a href="/wiki/North_Germanic_languages" title="North Germanic languages">North Germanic</a> language branch and is descended from <a href="/wiki/Old_Norse" title="Old Norse">Old Norse</a>, being most closely related to <a href="/wiki/Icelandic_language" title="Icelandic language">Icelandic</a>. Due to its geographic isolation, it has preserved more conservative grammatical features that have been lost in Danish, Norwegian and Swedish. It is the only language alongside Icelandic and <a href="/wiki/Elfdalian" title="Elfdalian">Elfdalian</a> to preserve the letter <a href="/wiki/Eth" title="Eth">Ð</a>, though unlike the others, it is not pronounced.
</p><p>Faroese sign language was officially adopted as a national language in 2017.<sup id="cite_ref-78" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-78">&#91;77&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Religion">Religion</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Faroe_Islands&amp;action=edit&amp;section=15" title="Edit section: Religion">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Religion_in_the_Faroe_Islands" title="Religion in the Faroe Islands">Religion in the Faroe Islands</a></div>
<p>According to the <i><a href="/wiki/F%C3%A6reyinga_saga" title="Færeyinga saga">Færeyinga saga</a></i>, <a href="/wiki/Sigmundur_Brestisson" title="Sigmundur Brestisson">Sigmundur Brestisson</a> brought Christianity to the islands in 999. However, archaeology at a site in Toftanes, <a href="/wiki/Leirv%C3%ADk" title="Leirvík">Leirvík</a>, named Bønhústoftin (English: prayer-house ruin) and over a dozen slabs from Ólansgarður in the small island of <a href="/wiki/Sk%C3%BAvoy" title="Skúvoy">Skúvoy</a> which in the main display encircled linear and outline crosses, suggest that <a href="/wiki/Celtic_Christianity" title="Celtic Christianity">Celtic Christianity</a> may have arrived at least 150 years earlier.<sup id="cite_ref-Bønhústoftin_79-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Bønhústoftin-79">&#91;78&#93;</a></sup> The Faroe Islands' Church <a href="/wiki/Protestant_Reformation" class="mw-redirect" title="Protestant Reformation">Reformation</a> was completed on 1 January 1540. According to official statistics from 2019, 79.7% of the Faroese population are members of the state church, the <a href="/wiki/Church_of_the_Faroe_Islands" title="Church of the Faroe Islands">Church of the Faroe Islands</a> (Fólkakirkjan), following a form of <a href="/wiki/Lutheranism" title="Lutheranism">Lutheranism</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-80" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-80">&#91;79&#93;</a></sup> The Fólkakirkjan became an independent church in 2007; previously it had been a diocese within the <a href="/wiki/Church_of_Denmark" title="Church of Denmark">Church of Denmark</a>. Faroese members of the clergy who have had historical importance include <a href="/wiki/Venceslaus_Ulricus_Hammershaimb" title="Venceslaus Ulricus Hammershaimb">Venceslaus Ulricus Hammershaimb</a> (1819–1909), <a href="/wiki/Fr%C3%AD%C3%B0rikur_Petersen" title="Fríðrikur Petersen">Fríðrikur Petersen</a> (1853–1917) and, perhaps most significantly, <a href="/wiki/J%C3%A1kup_Dahl" title="Jákup Dahl">Jákup Dahl</a> (1878–1944), who had a great influence in ensuring that the <a href="/wiki/Faroese_language" title="Faroese language">Faroese language</a> was spoken in the church instead of <a href="/wiki/Danish_language" title="Danish language">Danish</a>. Participation in churches is more prevalent among the Faroese population than among most other Scandinavians.
</p><p>In the late 1820s, the Christian <a href="/wiki/Evangelicalism" title="Evangelicalism">Evangelical</a> religious movement, the <a href="/wiki/Plymouth_Brethren" title="Plymouth Brethren">Plymouth Brethren</a>, was established in England. In 1865, a member of this movement, <a href="/wiki/William_Gibson_Sloan" title="William Gibson Sloan">William Gibson Sloan</a>, travelled to the Faroes from <a href="/wiki/Shetland" title="Shetland">Shetland</a>. At the turn of the 20th century, the Faroese Plymouth Brethren numbered thirty. Today, around 10% of the Faroese population are members of the Open Brethren community (<i>Brøðrasamkoman</i>). About 3% belong to the <a href="/wiki/Charismatic_Movement" class="mw-redirect" title="Charismatic Movement">Charismatic Movement</a>. There are several charismatic churches around the islands, the largest of which, called Keldan (The Spring), has about 200 to 300 members. About 2% belong to other Christian groups. The <a href="/wiki/Adventism" title="Adventism">Adventists</a> operate a private school in Tórshavn. <a href="/wiki/Jehovah%27s_Witnesses" title="Jehovah&#39;s Witnesses">Jehovah's Witnesses</a> also have four congregations with a total of 121 members. The Roman Catholic congregation has about 270 members and falls under the jurisdiction of Denmark's <a href="/wiki/Roman_Catholic_Diocese_of_Copenhagen" title="Roman Catholic Diocese of Copenhagen">Roman Catholic Diocese of Copenhagen</a>. The municipality of <a href="/wiki/T%C3%B3rshavn" title="Tórshavn">Tórshavn</a> has an old <a href="/wiki/Franciscans" title="Franciscans">Franciscan</a> school.
</p>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Kunoy,_Faroe_Islands_(2).JPG" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/86/Kunoy%2C_Faroe_Islands_%282%29.JPG/220px-Kunoy%2C_Faroe_Islands_%282%29.JPG" decoding="async" width="220" height="146" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/86/Kunoy%2C_Faroe_Islands_%282%29.JPG/330px-Kunoy%2C_Faroe_Islands_%282%29.JPG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/86/Kunoy%2C_Faroe_Islands_%282%29.JPG/440px-Kunoy%2C_Faroe_Islands_%282%29.JPG 2x" data-file-width="2560" data-file-height="1704" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Kunoy,_Faroe_Islands_(2).JPG" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Church in <a href="/wiki/Kunoy" title="Kunoy">Kunoy</a></div></div></div>
<p>Unlike <a href="/wiki/Denmark" title="Denmark">Denmark</a>, <a href="/wiki/Sweden" title="Sweden">Sweden</a> and <a href="/wiki/Iceland" title="Iceland">Iceland</a>, the Faroes have no organised <a href="/wiki/Heathenry_(new_religious_movement)" title="Heathenry (new religious movement)">Heathen</a> community.
</p><p>The best-known church buildings in the Faroe Islands include <a href="/wiki/T%C3%B3rshavn_Cathedral" title="Tórshavn Cathedral">Tórshavn Cathedral</a>, <a href="/wiki/Olaf_II_of_Norway" title="Olaf II of Norway">Olaf II of Norway</a>'s Church and the <a href="/wiki/Magnus_Cathedral" title="Magnus Cathedral">Magnus Cathedral</a> in <a href="/wiki/Kirkjub%C3%B8ur" title="Kirkjubøur">Kirkjubøur</a>; the Vesturkirkjan and the <a href="/wiki/Catholic_Church_in_the_Faroe_Islands#St_Mary&#39;s_Church_Today" title="Catholic Church in the Faroe Islands">St. Mary's Church</a>, both of which are situated in Tórshavn; the church of <a href="/wiki/F%C3%A1mjin" title="Fámjin">Fámjin</a>; the octagonal church in <a href="/wiki/Hald%C3%B3rsv%C3%ADk" title="Haldórsvík">Haldórsvík</a>; Christianskirkjan in <a href="/wiki/Klaksv%C3%ADk" title="Klaksvík">Klaksvík</a>; and also the two pictured here.
</p><p>In 1948, Victor Danielsen completed the first Bible translation into Faroese from different modern languages. Jacob Dahl and Kristian Osvald Viderø (Fólkakirkjan) completed the second translation in 1961. The latter was translated from the original Biblical languages (<a href="/wiki/Hebrew_language" title="Hebrew language">Hebrew</a> and <a href="/wiki/Greek_language" title="Greek language">Greek</a>) into Faroese.
</p><p>According to the 2011 Census, there were 33,018 Christians (95.44%), 23 Muslims (0.07%), 7 <a href="/wiki/Hindu" class="mw-redirect" title="Hindu">Hindus</a> (0.02%), 66 <a href="/wiki/Buddhism" title="Buddhism">Buddhists</a> (0.19%), 12 <a href="/wiki/Jews" title="Jews">Jews</a> (0.03%), 13 Baháʼís (0.04%), 3 <a href="/wiki/Sikhism" title="Sikhism">Sikhs</a> (0.01%), 149 others (0.43%), 85 with more than one belief (0.25%), and 1,397 with no religion (4.04%).<sup id="cite_ref-81" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-81">&#91;80&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Education">Education</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Faroe_Islands&amp;action=edit&amp;section=16" title="Edit section: Education">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Education_in_the_Faroe_Islands" title="Education in the Faroe Islands">Education in the Faroe Islands</a></div>
<p>The levels of education in the Faroe Islands are <a href="/wiki/Primary_education" title="Primary education">primary</a>, <a href="/wiki/Secondary_education" title="Secondary education">secondary</a> and <a href="/wiki/Higher_education" title="Higher education">higher education</a>. Most institutions are funded by the state; there are few private schools in the Faroe Islands. Education is compulsory for 9 years between the ages of 7 and 16.<sup id="cite_ref-educationfaroe_82-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-educationfaroe-82">&#91;81&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Compulsory education consists of seven years of primary education and two years of lower secondary education; it is public, free of charge, provided by the respective municipalities, and is called the Fólkaskúli in Faroese. The Fólkaskúli also provides optional preschool education as well as the tenth year of education that is a prerequisite to getting admitted to upper secondary education. Students that complete compulsory education are allowed to continue education in a <a href="/wiki/Vocational_school" title="Vocational school">vocational school</a>, where they can have job-specific training and education. Since the fishing industry is an important part of <a href="/wiki/Economy_of_the_Faroe_Islands" title="Economy of the Faroe Islands">Faroe Islands' economy</a>, maritime schools are an important part of Faroese education. Upon completion of the tenth year of Fólkaskúli, students can continue to upper secondary education which consists of several different types of schools. Higher education is offered at the <a href="/wiki/University_of_the_Faroe_Islands" title="University of the Faroe Islands">University of the Faroe Islands</a>; a part of Faroese youth moves abroad to pursue higher education, mainly in <a href="/wiki/Denmark" title="Denmark">Denmark</a>. Other forms of education comprise <a href="/wiki/Adult_education" title="Adult education">adult education</a> and music schools. The structure of the Faroese educational system bears resemblances with its Danish counterpart.<sup id="cite_ref-educationfaroe_82-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-educationfaroe-82">&#91;81&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In the 12th century, education was provided by the <a href="/wiki/Catholic_Church_in_the_Faroe_Islands" title="Catholic Church in the Faroe Islands">Catholic Church in the Faroe Islands</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Debes_2000_83-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Debes_2000-83">&#91;82&#93;</a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/Church_of_Denmark" title="Church of Denmark">Church of Denmark</a> took over education after the <a href="/wiki/Reformation_in_Denmark%E2%80%93Norway_and_Holstein" title="Reformation in Denmark–Norway and Holstein">Protestant Reformation</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Debes_2000,_chapter_2_84-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Debes_2000,_chapter_2-84">&#91;83&#93;</a></sup>
Modern educational institutions started operating in the last quarter of the nineteenth century and developed throughout the twentieth century. The status of the Faroese language in education was a significant issue for decades, until it was accepted as a language of instruction in 1938.<sup id="cite_ref-cultral_85-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-cultral-85">&#91;84&#93;</a></sup> Initially education was administered and regulated by Denmark.<sup id="cite_ref-cultral_85-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-cultral-85">&#91;84&#93;</a></sup> In 1979 responsibilities on educational issues started transferring to the Faroese authorities, a procedure which was completed in 2002.<sup id="cite_ref-cultral_85-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-cultral-85">&#91;84&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/Ministry_of_Education,_Research_and_Culture_(Faroe_Islands)" title="Ministry of Education, Research and Culture (Faroe Islands)">Ministry of Education, Research and Culture</a> has the jurisdiction of educational responsibility in the Faroe Islands.<sup id="cite_ref-government-education_86-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-government-education-86">&#91;85&#93;</a></sup> Since the Faroe Islands is a part of the <a href="/wiki/Danish_Realm" title="Danish Realm">Danish Realm</a>, education in the Faroe Islands is influenced and has similarities with the <a href="/wiki/Education_in_Denmark" title="Education in Denmark">Danish educational system</a>; there is an agreement on educational cooperation between the Faroe Islands and Denmark.<sup id="cite_ref-cultral_85-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-cultral-85">&#91;84&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-adult_87-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-adult-87">&#91;86&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-88" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-88">&#91;87&#93;</a></sup> In 2012 the public spending on education was 8.1% of <a href="/wiki/Gross_domestic_product" title="Gross domestic product">GDP</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-fin_89-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-fin-89">&#91;88&#93;</a></sup> The municipalities are responsible for the school buildings for children's education in Fólkaskúlin from age 1st grade to 9th or 10th grade (age 7 to 16).<sup id="cite_ref-Skúlaviðurskifti_90-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Skúlaviðurskifti-90">&#91;89&#93;</a></sup> In November 2013 1,615 people, or 6.8% of the total number of employees, were employed in the education sector.<sup id="cite_ref-fin_89-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-fin-89">&#91;88&#93;</a></sup> Of the 31,270 people aged 25 and above 1,717 (5.5%) have gained at least a <a href="/wiki/Master%27s_degree" title="Master&#39;s degree">master's degrees</a> or a <a href="/wiki/Doctor_of_Philosophy" title="Doctor of Philosophy">Ph.D.</a>, 8,428 (27%) have gained a <a href="/wiki/Bachelor_of_Science" title="Bachelor of Science">B.Sc.</a> or a <a href="/wiki/Diploma" title="Diploma">diploma</a>, 11,706 (37.4%) have finished upper secondary education while 9,419 (30.1%) has only finished primary school and have no other education.<sup id="cite_ref-91" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-91">&#91;90&#93;</a></sup> There is no data on literacy in the Faroe Islands, but the CIA Factbook states that it is probably as high as in <a href="/wiki/Denmark" title="Denmark">Denmark</a> proper, i.e. 99%.<sup id="cite_ref-92" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-92">&#91;91&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The majority of students in upper secondary schools are women, although men represent the majority in higher education institutions. In addition, most young Faroese people who relocate to other countries to study are women.<sup id="cite_ref-Studni2015_93-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Studni2015-93">&#91;92&#93;</a></sup> Out of 8,535 holders of <a href="/wiki/Bachelor_degrees" class="mw-redirect" title="Bachelor degrees">bachelor degrees</a>, 4,796 (56.2%) have had their education in the Faroe Islands, 2,724 (31.9%) in Denmark, 543 in both the Faroe Islands and Denmark, 94 (1.1%) in Norway, 80 in the United Kingdom and the rest in other countries.<sup id="cite_ref-educationpercountry_94-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-educationpercountry-94">&#91;93&#93;</a></sup> Out of 1,719 holders of master's degrees or PhDs, 1,249 (72.7%) have had their education in Denmark, 87 (5.1%) in the United Kingdom, 86 (5%) in both the Faroe Islands and Denmark, 64 (3.7%) in the Faroe Islands, 60 (3.5%) in Norway and the rest in other countries (mostly EU and Nordic).<sup id="cite_ref-educationpercountry_94-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-educationpercountry-94">&#91;93&#93;</a></sup> Since there is no medical school in the Faroe Islands, all medical students have to study abroad; as of 2013<sup class="plainlinks noexcerpt noprint asof-tag update" style="display:none;"><a class="external text" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Faroe_Islands&amp;action=edit">&#91;update&#93;</a></sup>, out of a total of 96 medical students, 76 studied in Denmark, 19 in <a href="/wiki/Poland" title="Poland">Poland</a>, and 1 in <a href="/wiki/Hungary" title="Hungary">Hungary</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Læknalesandi_95-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Læknalesandi-95">&#91;94&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Economy">Economy</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Faroe_Islands&amp;action=edit&amp;section=17" title="Edit section: Economy">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Economy_of_the_Faroe_Islands" title="Economy of the Faroe Islands">Economy of the Faroe Islands</a></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/Energy_in_the_Faroe_Islands" title="Energy in the Faroe Islands">Energy in the Faroe Islands</a></div>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:262px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Tree_map_exports_2009_Faeore_Islands.jpeg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c8/Tree_map_exports_2009_Faeore_Islands.jpeg/260px-Tree_map_exports_2009_Faeore_Islands.jpeg" decoding="async" width="260" height="203" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c8/Tree_map_exports_2009_Faeore_Islands.jpeg/390px-Tree_map_exports_2009_Faeore_Islands.jpeg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c8/Tree_map_exports_2009_Faeore_Islands.jpeg/520px-Tree_map_exports_2009_Faeore_Islands.jpeg 2x" data-file-width="1104" data-file-height="863" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Tree_map_exports_2009_Faeore_Islands.jpeg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Graphical depiction of Faroe Islands' product exports in 28 colour-coded categories</div></div></div>
<p>Economic troubles caused by a collapse of the Faroese fishing industry in the early 1990s brought high unemployment rates of 10 to 15% by the mid-1990s.<sup id="cite_ref-StatsFO_96-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-StatsFO-96">&#91;95&#93;</a></sup> Unemployment decreased in the later 1990s, down to about 6% at the end of 1998.<sup id="cite_ref-StatsFO_96-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-StatsFO-96">&#91;95&#93;</a></sup> By June 2008 unemployment had declined to 1.1%, before rising to 3.4% in early 2009.<sup id="cite_ref-StatsFO_96-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-StatsFO-96">&#91;95&#93;</a></sup> In December 2019<sup id="cite_ref-Hagstovan_unemployment_97-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Hagstovan_unemployment-97">&#91;96&#93;</a></sup> the unemployment reached a record low 0.9%. Nevertheless, the almost total dependence on fishing and <a href="/wiki/Fish_farming" title="Fish farming">fish farming</a> means that the economy remains vulnerable. One of the biggest private companies of the Faroe Islands is the salmon farming company <a href="/wiki/Bakkafrost" title="Bakkafrost">Bakkafrost</a>, which is the largest of the four salmon farming companies in the Faroe Islands<sup id="cite_ref-Fishupdate_98-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Fishupdate-98">&#91;97&#93;</a></sup> and the eighth biggest in the world.<sup id="cite_ref-99" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-99">&#91;98&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<div class="thumb tleft"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Faroe_Islands,_Bor%C3%B0oy,_Klaksv%C3%ADk_(3).jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a1/Faroe_Islands%2C_Bor%C3%B0oy%2C_Klaksv%C3%ADk_%283%29.jpg/220px-Faroe_Islands%2C_Bor%C3%B0oy%2C_Klaksv%C3%ADk_%283%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="146" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a1/Faroe_Islands%2C_Bor%C3%B0oy%2C_Klaksv%C3%ADk_%283%29.jpg/330px-Faroe_Islands%2C_Bor%C3%B0oy%2C_Klaksv%C3%ADk_%283%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a1/Faroe_Islands%2C_Bor%C3%B0oy%2C_Klaksv%C3%ADk_%283%29.jpg/440px-Faroe_Islands%2C_Bor%C3%B0oy%2C_Klaksv%C3%ADk_%283%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2144" data-file-height="1424" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Faroe_Islands,_Bor%C3%B0oy,_Klaksv%C3%ADk_(3).jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div><a href="/wiki/Klaksv%C3%ADk" title="Klaksvík">Klaksvík</a>, on the island of Borðoy, is the Faroe Islands' second-largest town.</div></div></div>
<p>In 2011, 13% of the Faroe Islands' national income consists of economic aid from <a href="/wiki/Denmark" title="Denmark">Denmark</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-100" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-100">&#91;99&#93;</a></sup> corresponding to roughly 5% of GDP.<sup id="cite_ref-101" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-101">&#91;100&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Since 2000, the government has fostered new information technology and business projects to attract new investment. The introduction of <a href="/wiki/Burger_King" title="Burger King">Burger King</a> in <a href="/wiki/T%C3%B3rshavn" title="Tórshavn">Tórshavn</a> was widely publicized as a sign of the globalization of <a href="/wiki/Culture_of_the_Faroe_Islands" title="Culture of the Faroe Islands">Faroese culture</a>. It remains to be seen whether these projects will succeed in broadening the islands' economic base. The islands have one of the lowest unemployment rates in Europe, but this should not necessarily be taken as a sign of a recovering economy, as many young students move to Denmark and other countries after leaving high school. This leaves a largely middle-aged and elderly population that may lack the skills and knowledge to fill newly developed positions on the Faroes. Nonetheless, in 2008 the Faroes were able to make a $52 million loan to <a href="/wiki/Iceland" title="Iceland">Iceland</a> in the wake of the <a href="/wiki/2008%E2%80%932011_Icelandic_financial_crisis" title="2008–2011 Icelandic financial crisis">2008 financial crisis</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-NYT_102-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-NYT-102">&#91;101&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On 5 August 2009, two opposition parties introduced a bill in the <i><a href="/wiki/L%C3%B8gting" title="Løgting">Løgting</a></i> to adopt the euro as the national currency, pending a referendum.<sup id="cite_ref-103" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-103">&#91;102&#93;</a></sup> The euro was not adopted.
</p>
 <div style="clear:left;"></div>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Transport">Transport</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Faroe_Islands&amp;action=edit&amp;section=18" title="Edit section: Transport">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Transport_in_the_Faroe_Islands" title="Transport in the Faroe Islands">Transport in the Faroe Islands</a></div>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Faroe_Islands,_Eysturoy,_road_from_Skipanes_to_Sy%C3%B0rug%C3%B8ta.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c2/Faroe_Islands%2C_Eysturoy%2C_road_from_Skipanes_to_Sy%C3%B0rug%C3%B8ta.jpg/220px-Faroe_Islands%2C_Eysturoy%2C_road_from_Skipanes_to_Sy%C3%B0rug%C3%B8ta.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="146" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c2/Faroe_Islands%2C_Eysturoy%2C_road_from_Skipanes_to_Sy%C3%B0rug%C3%B8ta.jpg/330px-Faroe_Islands%2C_Eysturoy%2C_road_from_Skipanes_to_Sy%C3%B0rug%C3%B8ta.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c2/Faroe_Islands%2C_Eysturoy%2C_road_from_Skipanes_to_Sy%C3%B0rug%C3%B8ta.jpg/440px-Faroe_Islands%2C_Eysturoy%2C_road_from_Skipanes_to_Sy%C3%B0rug%C3%B8ta.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1501" data-file-height="997" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Faroe_Islands,_Eysturoy,_road_from_Skipanes_to_Sy%C3%B0rug%C3%B8ta.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Shown here is the road from <a href="/wiki/Skipanes" title="Skipanes">Skipanes</a> to <a href="/wiki/Sy%C3%B0rug%C3%B8ta" title="Syðrugøta">Syðrugøta</a> on the island of <a href="/wiki/Eysturoy" title="Eysturoy">Eysturoy</a>.</div></div></div>
<p>By road, the main islands are connected by bridges and tunnels. Government-owned <a href="/wiki/Strandfaraskip_Landsins" title="Strandfaraskip Landsins">Strandfaraskip Landsins</a> provides public bus and ferry service to the main towns and villages. There are no railways.
</p><p>By air, <a href="/wiki/Scandinavian_Airlines" title="Scandinavian Airlines">Scandinavian Airlines</a> and the government-owned <a href="/wiki/Atlantic_Airways" title="Atlantic Airways">Atlantic Airways</a> both have scheduled international flights to <a href="/wiki/V%C3%A1gar_Airport" title="Vágar Airport">Vágar Airport</a>, the islands' only airport. Atlantic Airways also provides helicopter service to each of the islands. All civil aviation matters are controlled from the <a href="/wiki/Civil_Aviation_Administration_Denmark" title="Civil Aviation Administration Denmark">Civil Aviation Administration Denmark</a>.
</p><p>By sea, <a href="/wiki/Smyril_Line" title="Smyril Line">Smyril Line</a> operates a regular international passenger, car and freight service linking the Faroe Islands with <a href="/wiki/Sey%C3%B0isfj%C3%B6r%C3%B0ur" title="Seyðisfjörður">Seyðisfjörður</a>, Iceland and <a href="/wiki/Hirtshals" title="Hirtshals">Hirtshals</a>, Denmark.<sup id="cite_ref-104" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-104">&#91;103&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:New_smyril_11.56.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c9/New_smyril_11.56.jpg/220px-New_smyril_11.56.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="146" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c9/New_smyril_11.56.jpg/330px-New_smyril_11.56.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c9/New_smyril_11.56.jpg/440px-New_smyril_11.56.jpg 2x" data-file-width="640" data-file-height="426" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:New_smyril_11.56.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>The new ferry <a href="/wiki/MS_Smyril" title="MS Smyril">MS <i>Smyril</i></a> enters the Faroe Islands at <a href="/wiki/Krambatangi" title="Krambatangi">Krambatangi</a> ferry port in <a href="/wiki/Su%C3%B0uroy" title="Suðuroy">Suðuroy</a>, 2005</div></div></div>
<p>Because of the rugged terrain, road transport in the Faroe Islands was not as extensive as in other parts of the world. This has now changed, and the infrastructure has been developed extensively. Some 80 percent of the population of the islands is connected by <a href="/wiki/List_of_tunnels_of_the_Faroe_Islands" title="List of tunnels of the Faroe Islands">tunnels through the mountains and between the islands</a>, bridges and <a href="/wiki/Causeway" title="Causeway">causeways</a> that link together the three largest islands and three other islands to the northeast. While the other two large islands to the south, Sandoy and Suðuroy, are connected to the main area with <a href="/wiki/Ferry" title="Ferry">ferries</a>, the small islands Koltur and Stóra Dímun have no ferry connection, only a helicopter service. Other small islands—Mykines to the west, Kalsoy, Svínoy and Fugloy to the north, Hestur west of Streymoy, and Nólsoy east of Tórshavn—have smaller ferries and some of these islands also have a helicopter service.
</p><p>In February 2014 all the political parties of the <i>Løgting</i> agreed on making two new subsea tunnels, one between Streymoy and Eysturoy (the <a href="/wiki/Eysturoyartunnilin" title="Eysturoyartunnilin">Eysturoyartunnilin</a>) and one between Streymoy and Sandoy (<a href="/wiki/Sandoyartunnilin" title="Sandoyartunnilin">Sandoyartunnilin</a>). The plan was that both tunnels should open in 2021 and not be private.<sup id="cite_ref-105" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-105">&#91;104&#93;</a></sup> The work to dig the Eysturoy-tunnel started on 1 March 2016 above the village of <a href="/wiki/Hv%C3%ADtanes" title="Hvítanes">Hvítanes</a> near Tórshavn, and it opened for traffic in late 2020.<sup id="cite_ref-Grabbin_106-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Grabbin-106">&#91;105&#93;</a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/Sandoyartunnilin" title="Sandoyartunnilin">Sandoyartunnilin</a> is now expected to open in late 2023.
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Culture">Culture</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Faroe_Islands&amp;action=edit&amp;section=19" title="Edit section: Culture">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Culture_of_the_Faroe_Islands" title="Culture of the Faroe Islands">Culture of the Faroe Islands</a></div>
<p>The culture of the Faroe Islands has its roots in the <a href="/wiki/Nordic_countries" title="Nordic countries">Nordic</a> culture. The Faroe Islands were long isolated from the main cultural phases and movements that swept across parts of Europe. This means that they have maintained a great part of their traditional culture. The language spoken is <a href="/wiki/Faroese_language" title="Faroese language">Faroese</a>, which is one of three insular <a href="/wiki/North_Germanic_languages" title="North Germanic languages">North Germanic languages</a> descended from the <a href="/wiki/Old_Norse" title="Old Norse">Old Norse</a> language spoken in Scandinavia in the <a href="/wiki/Viking_Age" title="Viking Age">Viking Age</a>, the others being <a href="/wiki/Icelandic_language" title="Icelandic language">Icelandic</a> and the extinct <a href="/wiki/Norn_language" title="Norn language">Norn</a>, which is thought to have been mutually intelligible with Faroese. Until the 15th century, Faroese had a similar orthography to Icelandic and <a href="/wiki/Norwegian_language" title="Norwegian language">Norwegian</a>, but after the Reformation in 1538, the ruling <a href="/wiki/Norway" title="Norway">Norwegians</a> outlawed its use in schools, churches and official documents. Although a rich spoken tradition survived, for 300 years the language was not written down. This means that all poems and stories were handed down orally. These works were split into the following divisions: <i>sagnir</i> (historical), <i>ævintýr</i> (stories) and <i><a href="/wiki/Kv%C3%A6%C3%B0i" title="Kvæði">kvæði</a></i> (ballads), often set to music and the <a href="/wiki/Middle_Ages" title="Middle Ages">medieval</a> chain dance. These were eventually written down in the 19th century.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Faroese_literature">Faroese literature</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Faroe_Islands&amp;action=edit&amp;section=20" title="Edit section: Faroese literature">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Faroese_literature" title="Faroese literature">Faroese literature</a></div>
<div class="thumb tleft"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:172px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Faroe_stamp_364_rasmussen_and_skardi.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6f/Faroe_stamp_364_rasmussen_and_skardi.jpg/170px-Faroe_stamp_364_rasmussen_and_skardi.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="248" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6f/Faroe_stamp_364_rasmussen_and_skardi.jpg/255px-Faroe_stamp_364_rasmussen_and_skardi.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/6/6f/Faroe_stamp_364_rasmussen_and_skardi.jpg 2x" data-file-width="265" data-file-height="387" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Faroe_stamp_364_rasmussen_and_skardi.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div><a href="/wiki/Regin_%C3%AD_L%C3%AD%C3%B0" class="mw-redirect" title="Regin í Líð">Rasmus Rasmussen</a>, the writer who wrote the first novel in the <a href="/wiki/Faroese_language" title="Faroese language">Faroese language</a> (poetical name: Regin í Líð) and <a href="/wiki/S%C3%ADmun_av_Skar%C3%B0i" title="Símun av Skarði">Símun av Skarði</a>, the poet who wrote the Faroese national hymn</div></div></div>
<p>Faroese written literature has developed only in the past 100–200 years. This is mainly because of the islands' isolation, and also because the <a href="/wiki/Faroese_language" title="Faroese language">Faroese language</a> did not have a standardised writing system. The Danish language was also encouraged at the expense of Faroese. Nevertheless, the Faroes have produced several authors and poets. A rich centuries-old oral tradition of folk tales and Faroese folk songs accompanied the <a href="/wiki/Faroese_dance" class="mw-redirect" title="Faroese dance">Faroese chain dance</a>. The people learned these songs and stories by heart, and told or sang them to each other, teaching the younger generations too. This kind of literature was gathered in the 19th century and early 20th century. The Faroese folk songs, in Faroese called <i>kvæði</i>, are still in use although not so large-scale as earlier.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (April 2022)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>The first <a href="/wiki/List_of_novels_written_in_Faroese" title="List of novels written in Faroese">Faroese novel</a>, <i>Bábelstornið</i> by <a href="/wiki/Regin_%C3%AD_L%C3%AD%C3%B0" class="mw-redirect" title="Regin í Líð">Regin í Líð</a>, was published in 1909; the second novel was published 18 years later. In the period 1930 to 1940 a writer from the village Skálavík on Sandoy island, <a href="/wiki/He%C3%B0in_Br%C3%BA" title="Heðin Brú">Heðin Brú</a>, published three novels: <i>Lognbrá</i> (1930), <i>Fastatøkur</i> (1935) and <i>Feðgar á ferð</i> (English title: <i>The old man and his sons</i>) (1940). <i>Feðgar á ferð</i> has been translated into several other languages. Martin Joensen from <a href="/wiki/Sandv%C3%ADk" title="Sandvík">Sandvík</a> wrote about life on Faroese fishing vessels; he published the novels <i>Fiskimenn</i> (1946)<sup id="cite_ref-107" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-107">&#91;106&#93;</a></sup> and <i>Tað lýsir á landi</i> (1952).
</p><p>Well-known poets from the early 20th century are among others the two brothers from Tórshavn: <a href="/wiki/Hans_Andrias_Djurhuus" title="Hans Andrias Djurhuus">Hans Andrias Djurhuus</a> (1883–1951)<sup id="cite_ref-108" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-108">&#91;107&#93;</a></sup> and <a href="/wiki/Janus_Djurhuus" title="Janus Djurhuus">Janus Djurhuus</a> (1881–1948),<sup id="cite_ref-109" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-109">&#91;108&#93;</a></sup> other well known poets from this period and the mid 20th century are <a href="/wiki/Poul_F._Joensen" title="Poul F. Joensen">Poul F. Joensen</a> (1898–1970),<sup id="cite_ref-110" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-110">&#91;109&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Regin_Dahl" title="Regin Dahl">Regin Dahl</a> (1918–2007)<sup id="cite_ref-111" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-111">&#91;110&#93;</a></sup> and Tummas Napoleon Djurhuus (1928–71).<sup id="cite_ref-112" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-112">&#91;111&#93;</a></sup> Their poems are popular even today and can be found in Faroese song books and school books. <a href="/wiki/Jens_Pauli_Heinesen" title="Jens Pauli Heinesen">Jens Pauli Heinesen</a> (1932–2011), a school teacher from <a href="/wiki/Sandav%C3%A1gur" title="Sandavágur">Sandavágur</a>, was the most productive Faroese novelist, he published 17 novels. <a href="/wiki/Steinbj%C3%B8rn_B._Jacobsen" title="Steinbjørn B. Jacobsen">Steinbjørn B. Jacobsen</a> (1937–2012), a schoolteacher from <a href="/wiki/Sandv%C3%ADk" title="Sandvík">Sandvík</a>, wrote short stories, plays, children's books and even novels. Most Faroese writers write in Faroese; two exceptions are <a href="/wiki/William_Heinesen" title="William Heinesen">William Heinesen</a> (1900–91) and <a href="/wiki/J%C3%B8rgen-Frantz_Jacobsen" title="Jørgen-Frantz Jacobsen">Jørgen-Frantz Jacobsen</a> (1900–38).
</p><p>Women were not so visible in the early Faroese literature except for <a href="/wiki/Helena_Patursson" title="Helena Patursson">Helena Patursson</a> (1864–1916), but in the last decades of the 20th century and in the beginning of the 21st century female writers like <a href="/wiki/Ebba_Hentze" title="Ebba Hentze">Ebba Hentze</a> (born 1933) wrote children's books, short stories, etc. <a href="/wiki/Gu%C3%B0ri%C3%B0_Helmsdal" title="Guðrið Helmsdal">Guðrið Helmsdal</a> published the first modernistic collection of poems, <i>Lýtt lot</i>, in 1963, which at the same time was the first collection of Faroese poems written by a woman.<sup id="cite_ref-113" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-113">&#91;112&#93;</a></sup> Her daughter, <a href="/wiki/Rakel_Helmsdal" title="Rakel Helmsdal">Rakel Helmsdal</a> (born 1966), is also a writer, best known for her children's books, for which she has won several prizes and nominations. Other female writers are the novelists <a href="/wiki/Oddv%C3%B8r_Johansen" title="Oddvør Johansen">Oddvør Johansen</a> (born 1941), <a href="/wiki/Bergt%C3%B3ra_Hanusard%C3%B3ttir" title="Bergtóra Hanusardóttir">Bergtóra Hanusardóttir</a> (born 1946) and novelist/children's books writers <a href="/wiki/Marianna_Debes_Dahl" title="Marianna Debes Dahl">Marianna Debes Dahl</a> (born 1947), and <a href="/wiki/S%C3%B3lrun_Michelsen" class="mw-redirect" title="Sólrun Michelsen">Sólrun Michelsen</a> (born 1948). Other modern Faroese writers include Gunnar Hoydal (born 1941), <a href="/wiki/Hanus_Kamban" title="Hanus Kamban">Hanus Kamban</a> (born 1942), <a href="/wiki/J%C3%B3gvan_Isaksen" title="Jógvan Isaksen">Jógvan Isaksen</a> (born 1950), <a href="/wiki/J%C3%B3anes_Nielsen" title="Jóanes Nielsen">Jóanes Nielsen</a> (born 1953), Tóroddur Poulsen and <a href="/wiki/Carl_J%C3%B3han_Jensen" title="Carl Jóhan Jensen">Carl Jóhan Jensen</a> (born 1957). Some of these writers have been nominated for the <a href="/wiki/Nordic_Council%27s_Literature_Prize" class="mw-redirect" title="Nordic Council&#39;s Literature Prize">Nordic Council's Literature Prize</a> two to six times, but have never won it. The only Faroese writer who writes in Faroese who has won the prize is the poet <a href="/wiki/R%C3%B3i_Patursson" title="Rói Patursson">Rói Patursson</a> (born 1947), who won the prize in 1986 for <i>Líkasum</i>.<sup id="cite_ref-114" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-114">&#91;113&#93;</a></sup> In 2007 the first
ever <a href="/wiki/Faroese_language" title="Faroese language">Faroese</a>/German anthology "From <a href="/wiki/Janus_Djurhuus" title="Janus Djurhuus">Janus Djurhuus</a> to <a href="/w/index.php?title=T%C3%B3roddur_Poulsen&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Tóroddur Poulsen (page does not exist)">Tóroddur Poulsen</a> – Faroese Poetry during 100 Years", edited by <a href="/wiki/Paul_Alfred_Kleinert" title="Paul Alfred Kleinert">Paul Alfred Kleinert</a>, including a short history of Faroese literature was published in Leipzig,.
</p><p>In the 21st century, some new writers had success in the Faroe Islands and abroad. <a href="/wiki/B%C3%A1r%C3%B0ur_Oskarsson" title="Bárður Oskarsson">Bárður Oskarsson</a> (born 1972) is a children's book writer and illustrator; his books won prizes in the Faroes, Germany and the <a href="/wiki/West_Nordic_Council%27s_Children_and_Youth_Literature_Prize" title="West Nordic Council&#39;s Children and Youth Literature Prize">West Nordic Council's Children and Youth Literature Prize</a> (2006). Though not born in the Faroe Islands, Matthew Landrum, an American poet and editor for <i><a href="/wiki/Structo" title="Structo">Structo</a></i> magazine, has written a collection of poems about the Islands. <a href="/wiki/Sissal_Kampmann" title="Sissal Kampmann">Sissal Kampmann</a> (born 1974) won the Danish literary prize <a href="/wiki/Klaus_Rifbjerg%27s_Debutant_Prize" class="mw-redirect" title="Klaus Rifbjerg&#39;s Debutant Prize">Klaus Rifbjerg's Debutant Prize</a> (2012), and Rakel Helmsdal has won Faroese and Icelandic awards; she has been nominated for the <a href="/wiki/West_Nordic_Council%27s_Children_and_Youth_Literature_Prize" title="West Nordic Council&#39;s Children and Youth Literature Prize">West Nordic Council's Children and Youth Literature Prize</a> and the Children and Youth Literature Prize of the Nordic Council (representing Iceland, wrote the book together with and Icelandic and a Swedish writer/illustrator). <a href="/wiki/Marjun_Syderb%C3%B8_Kjeln%C3%A6s" title="Marjun Syderbø Kjelnæs">Marjun Syderbø Kjelnæs</a> (born 1974) had success with her first novel <i>Skriva í sandin</i> for teenagers; the book was awarded and nominated both in the Faroes and in other countries. She won the <a href="/wiki/Nordic_Children%27s_Book_Prize" title="Nordic Children&#39;s Book Prize">Nordic Children's Book Prize</a> (2011) for this book, <a href="/wiki/International_Youth_Library" title="International Youth Library">White Raven Deutsche Jugendbibliothek</a> (2011) and nominated the <a href="/wiki/West_Nordic_Council%27s_Children_and_Youth_Literature_Prize" title="West Nordic Council&#39;s Children and Youth Literature Prize">West Nordic Council's Children and Youth Literature Prize</a> and the Children and Youth Literature Prize of the Nordic Council (2013).<sup id="cite_ref-115" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-115">&#91;114&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Music">Music</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Faroe_Islands&amp;action=edit&amp;section=21" title="Edit section: Music">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Music_of_the_Faroe_Islands" title="Music of the Faroe Islands">Music of the Faroe Islands</a></div>
<p>The Faroe Islands have an active music scene, with live music being a regular part of the Islands' life and many Faroese being proficient at a number of instruments. Multiple Danish Music Award winner <a href="/wiki/Teitur_Lassen" title="Teitur Lassen">Teitur Lassen</a> calls the Faroes home and is arguably the islands' most internationally well-known musical export.
</p><p>The Islands have their own <a href="/wiki/Orchestra" title="Orchestra">orchestra</a> (the classical ensemble <i>Aldubáran</i>) and many different choirs; the best-known of these is <i>Havnarkórið</i>. The best-known local Faroese composers are <a href="/wiki/Sunleif_Rasmussen" title="Sunleif Rasmussen">Sunleif Rasmussen</a> and <a href="/wiki/Kristian_Blak" title="Kristian Blak">Kristian Blak</a>, who is also head of the record company <a href="/wiki/Tutl" class="mw-redirect" title="Tutl">Tutl</a>. The first Faroese opera was by Sunleif Rasmussen. It is entitled <i>Í Óðamansgarði</i> (The Madman's Garden) and was premiered on 12 October 2006 at the Nordic House. The opera is based on a short story by the writer <a href="/wiki/William_Heinesen" title="William Heinesen">William Heinesen</a>.
</p><p>Young Faroese musicians who have gained much popularity recently are <a href="/wiki/Eiv%C3%B8r_P%C3%A1lsd%C3%B3ttir" class="mw-redirect" title="Eivør Pálsdóttir">Eivør Pálsdóttir</a>, Anna Katrin Egilstrøð, Lena (Lena Andersen), <a href="/wiki/H%C3%B8gni_Reistrup" title="Høgni Reistrup">Høgni Reistrup</a>, <a href="/wiki/H%C3%B8gni_Lisberg" title="Høgni Lisberg">Høgni Lisberg</a>, HEIÐRIK (<a href="/wiki/Hei%C3%B0rik_%C3%A1_Heygum" class="mw-redirect" title="Heiðrik á Heygum">Heiðrik á Heygum</a>), <a href="/wiki/Gu%C3%B0ri%C3%B0_Hansd%C3%B3ttir" title="Guðrið Hansdóttir">Guðrið Hansdóttir</a> and <a href="/wiki/Brandur_Enni" title="Brandur Enni">Brandur Enni</a>.
</p><p>Well-known bands include <a href="/wiki/T%C3%BDr_(band)" title="Týr (band)">Týr</a>, Gestir, <a href="/wiki/Hamfer%C3%B0" title="Hamferð">Hamferð</a>, <a href="/wiki/The_Ghost_(Faroese_band)" title="The Ghost (Faroese band)">The Ghost</a>, <a href="/wiki/Boys_in_a_Band" title="Boys in a Band">Boys in a Band</a>, ORKA, <a href="https://fo.wikipedia.org/wiki/200_(rokkb%C3%B3lkur)" class="extiw" title="fo:200 (rokkbólkur)">200</a>, Grandma's Basement, <a href="/wiki/Sic_(band)" title="Sic (band)">SIC</a>, and the former band Clickhaze.
</p><p>The festival of contemporary and classical music, <i>Summartónar</i>, is held each summer. The <a href="/wiki/G!_Festival" title="G! Festival">G! Festival</a> in <a href="/wiki/Nor%C3%B0rag%C3%B8ta" title="Norðragøta">Norðragøta</a> in July and <a href="/wiki/Summarfestivalurin" title="Summarfestivalurin">Summarfestivalurin</a> in <a href="/wiki/Klaksv%C3%ADk" title="Klaksvík">Klaksvík</a> in August are both large, open-air music festivals for popular music with both local and international musicians participating. Havnar Jazzfelag was established 21 November 1975, and is still active. Currently Havnar Jazzfelag is arranging VetrarJazz amongst other jazz-festivals in The Faroe Islands.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="The_Nordic_House_in_the_Faroe_Islands">The Nordic House in the Faroe Islands</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Faroe_Islands&amp;action=edit&amp;section=22" title="Edit section: The Nordic House in the Faroe Islands">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>The <a href="/wiki/Nordic_House_in_the_Faroe_Islands" title="Nordic House in the Faroe Islands">Nordic House in the Faroe Islands</a> (<a href="/wiki/Faroese_language" title="Faroese language">Faroese</a>: <i lang="fo">Norðurlandahúsið</i>) is the most important cultural institution in the Faroes. Its aim is to support and promote <a href="/wiki/Scandinavia" title="Scandinavia">Scandinavian</a> and Faroese culture, locally and in the Nordic region. <a href="/wiki/Erlendur_Patursson" title="Erlendur Patursson">Erlendur Patursson</a> (1913–86), Faroese member of the <a href="/wiki/Nordic_Council" title="Nordic Council">Nordic Council</a>, raised the idea of a Nordic cultural house in the Faroe Islands. A Nordic competition for architects was held in 1977, in which 158 architects participated. Winners were Ola Steen from <a href="/wiki/Norway" title="Norway">Norway</a> and Kolbrún Ragnarsdóttir from <a href="/wiki/Iceland" title="Iceland">Iceland</a>. By staying true to <a href="/wiki/Folklore" title="Folklore">folklore</a>, the architects built the Nordic House to resemble an enchanted hill of <a href="/wiki/Elf" title="Elf">elves</a>. The house opened in <a href="/wiki/T%C3%B3rshavn" title="Tórshavn">Tórshavn</a> in 1983. The Nordic House is a cultural organization under the Nordic Council. The Nordic House is run by a steering committee of eight, of whom three are Faroese and five from other Nordic countries. There is also a local advisory body of fifteen members, representing Faroese cultural organizations. The House is managed by a director appointed by the steering committee for a four-year term.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Traditional_food">Traditional food</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Faroe_Islands&amp;action=edit&amp;section=23" title="Edit section: Traditional food">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Faroese_cuisine" title="Faroese cuisine">Faroese cuisine</a></div>
<p>Traditional Faroese food is mainly based on meat, seafood and potatoes and uses few fresh vegetables. Mutton of the <a href="/wiki/Faroe_sheep" title="Faroe sheep">Faroe sheep</a> is the basis of many meals, and one of the most popular treats is <i><a href="/wiki/Skerpikj%C3%B8t" title="Skerpikjøt">skerpikjøt</a></i>, well aged, wind-dried mutton, which is quite chewy. The drying shed, known as a <i>hjallur</i>, is a standard feature in many Faroese homes, particularly in the small towns and villages. Other traditional foods are <i>ræst kjøt</i> (semi-dried mutton) and <i>ræstur fiskur</i>, matured fish. Another Faroese specialty is <i><a href="/wiki/Tv%C3%B8st_og_spik" title="Tvøst og spik">tvøst og spik</a></i>, made from <a href="/wiki/Pilot_whale" title="Pilot whale">pilot whale</a> meat and <a href="/wiki/Blubber" title="Blubber">blubber</a>. (A parallel meat/fat dish made with <a href="/wiki/Offal" title="Offal">offal</a> is <i><a href="/wiki/Garnat%C3%A1lg" title="Garnatálg">garnatálg</a></i>.) The tradition of consuming meat and blubber from pilot whales arises from the fact that a single kill can provide many meals. Fresh fish also features strongly in the traditional local diet, as do <a href="/wiki/Seabird" title="Seabird">seabirds</a>, such as <a href="/wiki/Faroese_puffin" class="mw-redirect" title="Faroese puffin">Faroese puffins</a>, and their eggs. Dried fish is also commonly eaten.
</p>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Dog_as_truck_driver.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b0/Dog_as_truck_driver.jpg/220px-Dog_as_truck_driver.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="164" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b0/Dog_as_truck_driver.jpg/330px-Dog_as_truck_driver.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b0/Dog_as_truck_driver.jpg/440px-Dog_as_truck_driver.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1139" data-file-height="850" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Dog_as_truck_driver.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Truck delivering <a href="/wiki/Chocolate" title="Chocolate">chocolate</a> in the Faroe Islands</div></div></div>
<p>There are two breweries in the Faroe Islands. The first brewery is called <a href="/wiki/F%C3%B6roya_Bj%C3%B3r" title="Föroya Bjór">Föroya Bjór</a> and has produced beer since 1888 with exports mainly to Iceland and Denmark. The second brewery is called Okkara Bryggjarí and was founded in 2010. A local specialty is <i>fredrikk</i>, a special brew made in <a href="/wiki/N%C3%B3lsoy" title="Nólsoy">Nólsoy</a>. Production of hard alcohol such as <a href="/wiki/Snaps" title="Snaps">snaps</a> is forbidden in the Faroe Islands, hence the Faroese <a href="/wiki/Akvavit" title="Akvavit">akvavit</a> is produced abroad.
</p><p>Since the friendly British occupation, the Faroese have been fond of British food, in particular British-style chocolate such as <a href="/wiki/Cadbury_Dairy_Milk" title="Cadbury Dairy Milk">Cadbury Dairy Milk</a>, which is found in many of the island's shops.<sup id="cite_ref-116" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-116">&#91;115&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Whaling">Whaling</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Faroe_Islands&amp;action=edit&amp;section=24" title="Edit section: Whaling">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Whaling_in_the_Faroe_Islands" title="Whaling in the Faroe Islands">Whaling in the Faroe Islands</a></div>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Whaling_in_the_Faroe_Islands_in_August_2012.JPG" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5c/Whaling_in_the_Faroe_Islands_in_August_2012.JPG/220px-Whaling_in_the_Faroe_Islands_in_August_2012.JPG" decoding="async" width="220" height="165" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5c/Whaling_in_the_Faroe_Islands_in_August_2012.JPG/330px-Whaling_in_the_Faroe_Islands_in_August_2012.JPG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5c/Whaling_in_the_Faroe_Islands_in_August_2012.JPG/440px-Whaling_in_the_Faroe_Islands_in_August_2012.JPG 2x" data-file-width="4608" data-file-height="3456" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Whaling_in_the_Faroe_Islands_in_August_2012.JPG" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Boats driving a pod of pilot whales into a bay of <a href="/wiki/Su%C3%B0uroy" title="Suðuroy">Suðuroy</a> in 2012</div></div></div>
<p>There are records of drive hunts in the Faroe Islands dating from 1584.<sup id="cite_ref-117" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-117">&#91;116&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Whaling_in_the_Faroe_Islands" title="Whaling in the Faroe Islands">Whaling in the Faroe Islands</a> is regulated by Faroese authorities but not by the <a href="/wiki/International_Whaling_Commission" title="International Whaling Commission">International Whaling Commission</a> as there are disagreements about the commission's legal authority to regulate <a href="/wiki/Cetacea" title="Cetacea">cetacean</a> hunts. Hundreds of <a href="/wiki/Long-finned_pilot_whale" title="Long-finned pilot whale">long-finned pilot whales</a> (<i>Globicephala melaena</i>) could be killed in a year, mainly during the summer. The hunts, called <i>grindadráp</i> in Faroese, are non-commercial and are organized on a community level; anyone can participate. When a whale pod by chance is spotted near land the participating hunters first surround the pilot whales with a wide semicircle of boats and then slowly and quietly begin to drive the whales towards the chosen authorised bay.<sup id="cite_ref-Home_118-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Home-118">&#91;117&#93;</a></sup> When a pod of whales has been stranded the killing is begun.
</p><p>Faroese animal welfare legislation, which also applies to whaling, requires that animals are killed as quickly and with as little suffering as possible. A regulation spinal lance is used to sever the <a href="/wiki/Spinal_cord" title="Spinal cord">spinal cord</a>, which also severs the major blood supply to the brain, ensuring both loss of consciousness and death within seconds. The spinal lance has been introduced as preferred standard equipment for killing pilot whales and has been shown to reduce killing time to 1–2 seconds.<sup id="cite_ref-Home_118-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Home-118">&#91;117&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>This "grindadráp" is legal and provides food for many people in the Faroe Islands.<sup id="cite_ref-119" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-119">&#91;118&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-120" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-120">&#91;119&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-121" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-121">&#91;120&#93;</a></sup> However, a study has found whale meat and blubber to currently be contaminated with <a href="/wiki/Mercury_(element)" title="Mercury (element)">mercury</a> and not recommended for human consumption, as too much may cause such adverse health effects as birth defects of the nervous system, high blood pressure, damaged immune system, increased risk for developing <a href="/wiki/Parkinson%27s_disease" title="Parkinson&#39;s disease">Parkinson's disease</a>, <a href="/wiki/Hypertension" title="Hypertension">hypertension</a>, <a href="/wiki/Arteriosclerosis" title="Arteriosclerosis">arteriosclerosis</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Diabetes_mellitus_type_2" class="mw-redirect" title="Diabetes mellitus type 2">Diabetes mellitus type 2</a>:
</p>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r996844942">.mw-parser-output .templatequote{overflow:hidden;margin:1em 0;padding:0 40px}.mw-parser-output .templatequote .templatequotecite{line-height:1.5em;text-align:left;padding-left:1.6em;margin-top:0}</style><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>Therefore we recommend that adults eat no more than one to two meals a month. Women who plan to become pregnant within three months, pregnant women, and nursing women should abstain from eating pilot whale meat. Pilot whale liver and kidneys should not be eaten at all.<sup id="cite_ref-122" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-122">&#91;121&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<p>Animal rights groups such as the <a href="/wiki/Sea_Shepherd_Conservation_Society" title="Sea Shepherd Conservation Society">Sea Shepherd Conservation Society</a> criticize it as being cruel and unnecessary, since it is no longer necessary as a food source for the Faroese people.
</p><p>The sustainability of the Faroese pilot whale hunt has been discussed, but with a long-term average catch of around 800 pilot whales on the Faroe Islands a year the hunt is not considered to have a significant impact on the pilot whale population. There are an estimated 128,000 pilot whales in the Northeast Atlantic, and Faroese whaling is therefore considered a sustainable catch by the Faroese government.<sup id="cite_ref-123" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-123">&#91;122&#93;</a></sup> Annual records of whale drives and strandings of pilot whales and other small cetaceans provide over 400 years of documentation, including statistics, and represents one of the most comprehensive historical records of wildlife utilization anywhere in the world.<sup id="cite_ref-Home_118-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Home-118">&#91;117&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On 12 September 2021, a <a href="/wiki/Pod_of_whales" class="mw-redirect" title="Pod of whales">super-pod</a> of over 1,420 <a href="/wiki/Atlantic_white-sided_dolphin" title="Atlantic white-sided dolphin">white-sided dolphins</a> were killed,<sup id="cite_ref-124" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-124">&#91;123&#93;</a></sup> an event that has caused significant debate in the Faroe Islands and internationally.<sup id="cite_ref-125" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-125">&#91;124&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-126" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-126">&#91;125&#93;</a></sup> The UK Government declined to suspend its free-trade agreement with the Faroese, having been called upon by conservationists to do so.<sup id="cite_ref-127" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-127">&#91;126&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Sports">Sports</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Faroe_Islands&amp;action=edit&amp;section=25" title="Edit section: Sports">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>The Faroe Islands have competed in every biennial <a href="/wiki/Island_Games" title="Island Games">Island Games</a> since they were established in 1985. The games were hosted by the islands in 1989 and Faroes won the <a href="/wiki/2009_Island_Games" title="2009 Island Games">Island Games in 2009</a>.
</p>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:172px;"><a href="/wiki/File:P%C3%A1l_Joensen_2014.JPG" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6c/P%C3%A1l_Joensen_2014.JPG/170px-P%C3%A1l_Joensen_2014.JPG" decoding="async" width="170" height="198" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6c/P%C3%A1l_Joensen_2014.JPG/255px-P%C3%A1l_Joensen_2014.JPG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6c/P%C3%A1l_Joensen_2014.JPG/340px-P%C3%A1l_Joensen_2014.JPG 2x" data-file-width="2479" data-file-height="2894" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:P%C3%A1l_Joensen_2014.JPG" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div><a href="/wiki/P%C3%A1l_Joensen" title="Pál Joensen">Pál Joensen</a>, Faroese swimmer</div></div></div>
<p><a href="/wiki/Association_football" title="Association football">Football</a> is by far the biggest sports activity on the islands, with 7,000 registered players out of the whole population of 52,000. Ten football teams contest the <a href="/wiki/Faroe_Islands_Premier_League" title="Faroe Islands Premier League">Faroe Islands Premier League</a>, currently ranked 51st by <a href="/wiki/Uefa_league_coefficient" class="mw-redirect" title="Uefa league coefficient">UEFA's League coefficient</a>. The Faroe Islands are a full member of <a href="/wiki/UEFA" title="UEFA">UEFA</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Faroe_Islands_national_football_team" title="Faroe Islands national football team">Faroe Islands national football team</a> competes in the <a href="/wiki/UEFA_European_Football_Championship" class="mw-redirect" title="UEFA European Football Championship">UEFA European Football Championship</a> qualifiers. The Faroe Islands is also a full member of <a href="/wiki/FIFA" title="FIFA">FIFA</a> and therefore the Faroe Islands football team also competes in the <a href="/wiki/FIFA_World_Cup" title="FIFA World Cup">FIFA World Cup</a> qualifiers. The Faroe Islands won its <a href="/wiki/List_of_Faroe_Islands_national_football_team_results" class="mw-redirect" title="List of Faroe Islands national football team results">first ever competitive match</a> when the team defeated <a href="/wiki/Austria_national_football_team" title="Austria national football team">Austria</a> 1–0 in a UEFA Euro 1992 qualifying.
</p><p>The nation's biggest success in football came in 2014 after defeating Greece 1–0, a result that was considered "the biggest shock of all time" in football<sup id="cite_ref-128" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-128">&#91;127&#93;</a></sup> thanks to a 169-place distance between the teams in the <a href="/wiki/FIFA_World_Rankings" class="mw-redirect" title="FIFA World Rankings">FIFA World Rankings</a> when the match was played. The team climbed 82 places to 105 on the FIFA ranking after the 1–0 win against Greece.<sup id="cite_ref-129" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-129">&#91;128&#93;</a></sup> The team went on to defeat Greece again on 13 June 2015 by a score of 2–1. On 9 July 2015 the national football team of the Faroes climbed another 28 places up on the FIFA ranking.<sup id="cite_ref-130" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-130">&#91;129&#93;</a></sup> Recently, Faroe Islands had achieved another famous victory, as they beat <a href="/wiki/Turkey_national_football_team" title="Turkey national football team">Turkey</a> 2–1 in the <a href="/wiki/2022%E2%80%9323_UEFA_Nations_League_C" title="2022–23 UEFA Nations League C">2022–23 UEFA Nations League C</a>, although this shock win didn't prevent Turkey from achieving promotion to <a href="/w/index.php?title=2023%E2%80%9324_UEFA_Nations_League_B&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="2023–24 UEFA Nations League B (page does not exist)">League B</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-131" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-131">&#91;130&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/Faroe_Islands_men%27s_national_handball_team" title="Faroe Islands men&#39;s national handball team">Faroe Islands men's national handball team</a> won the first two editions of the <a href="/wiki/IHF_Emerging_Nations_Championship" title="IHF Emerging Nations Championship">IHF Emerging Nations Championship</a>, in 2015, and 2017.
</p><p>The Faroe Islands are a full member of <a href="/wiki/FINA" class="mw-redirect" title="FINA">FINA</a> and compete under their own flag at World Championships, European Championships and World Cup events. The Faroese swimmer <a href="/wiki/P%C3%A1l_Joensen" title="Pál Joensen">Pál Joensen</a> (born 1990) won a bronze medal at the <a href="/wiki/2012_FINA_World_Swimming_Championships_(25_m)" title="2012 FINA World Swimming Championships (25 m)">2012 FINA World Swimming Championships (25 m)</a><sup id="cite_ref-132" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-132">&#91;131&#93;</a></sup> and four silver medals at the European Championships (<a href="/wiki/2010_European_Aquatics_Championships" title="2010 European Aquatics Championships">2010</a>, <a href="/wiki/2013_European_Short_Course_Swimming_Championships" title="2013 European Short Course Swimming Championships">2013</a> and <a href="/wiki/2014_European_Aquatics_Championships" title="2014 European Aquatics Championships">2014</a>),<sup id="cite_ref-133" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-133">&#91;132&#93;</a></sup> all medals won in the men's longest and second longest distance the 1500 and 800 metre freestyle, short and long course. The Faroe Islands also compete in the <a href="/wiki/Faroe_Islands_at_the_Paralympics" title="Faroe Islands at the Paralympics">Paralympics</a> and have won 1 gold, 7 silver, and 5 bronze medals since the <a href="/wiki/1984_Summer_Paralympics" title="1984 Summer Paralympics">1984 Summer Paralympics</a>.
</p><p>Two Faroese athletes have competed at the Olympics, but under the <a href="/wiki/Denmark_at_the_Olympics" title="Denmark at the Olympics">Danish flag</a>, since the Olympic Committee does not allow the Faroe Islands to compete under its own flag. The two Faroese who have competed are the swimmer <a href="/wiki/P%C3%A1l_Joensen" title="Pál Joensen">Pál Joensen</a> in 2012 and the rower <a href="/wiki/Katrin_Olsen" title="Katrin Olsen">Katrin Olsen</a>. She competed at the <a href="/wiki/Rowing_at_the_2008_Summer_Olympics" title="Rowing at the 2008 Summer Olympics">2008 Summer Olympics</a> in double sculler light weight together with <a href="/wiki/Juliane_Rasmussen" title="Juliane Rasmussen">Juliane Rasmussen</a>. Another Faroese rower, who is a member of the Danish National rowing team, is <a href="/wiki/Sverri_Sandberg_Nielsen" title="Sverri Sandberg Nielsen">Sverri Sandberg Nielsen</a>, who currently competes in single sculler, heavy weight, he has also competed in double sculler. He is the current Danish record holder in the men's indoor rowing, heavy weight; he broke a nine-year-old record in January 2015<sup id="cite_ref-Sportschef_134-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Sportschef-134">&#91;133&#93;</a></sup> and improved it in January 2016.<sup id="cite_ref-Danmarkarmeistari_135-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Danmarkarmeistari-135">&#91;134&#93;</a></sup> He has also competed at the <a href="/wiki/2015_World_Rowing_Championships" title="2015 World Rowing Championships">2015 World Rowing Championships</a> making it to the semifinal; he competed at the 2015 World Rowing Championship under-23 and made it to the final where he placed fourth.<sup id="cite_ref-World_Rowing_136-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-World_Rowing-136">&#91;135&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The Faroe Islands applied to the IOC for full Faroese membership in 1984, but as of 2017<sup class="plainlinks noexcerpt noprint asof-tag update" style="display:none;"><a class="external text" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Faroe_Islands&amp;action=edit">&#91;update&#93;</a></sup> the Faroe Islands are still not a member of the IOC. At the <a href="/wiki/2015_European_Games" title="2015 European Games">2015 European Games</a> in Baku, Azerbaijan, the Faroe Islands were not allowed to compete under the Faroese flag; they were, however, allowed to compete under the <a href="/wiki/Ligue_Europ%C3%A9enne_de_Natation" class="mw-redirect" title="Ligue Européenne de Natation">Ligue Européenne de Natation</a> flag. Before this, the Faroese prime minister <a href="/wiki/Kaj_Leo_Holm_Johannesen" class="mw-redirect" title="Kaj Leo Holm Johannesen">Kaj Leo Holm Johannesen</a> had a meeting with the IOC president <a href="/wiki/Thomas_Bach" title="Thomas Bach">Thomas Bach</a> in Lausanne on 21 May 2015 to discuss Faroese membership in the IOC.<sup id="cite_ref-137" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-137">&#91;136&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-138" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-138">&#91;137&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Faroese people are very active in sports; they have domestic competitions in football, handball, volleyball, badminton, swimming, outdoor rowing (<a href="/w/index.php?title=Faroese_kappr%C3%B3%C3%B0ur&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Faroese kappróður (page does not exist)">Faroese kappróður</a>) and indoor rowing in rowing machines, horse riding, shooting, table tennis, judo, golf, tennis, archery, gymnastics, <a href="/wiki/Tour_of_Faroe_Islands" title="Tour of Faroe Islands">cycling</a>, triathlon, running, and other competitions in athletics.<sup id="cite_ref-139" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-139">&#91;138&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>During 2014, the Faroe Islands was given the opportunity to compete in the Electronic Sports European Championship (ESEC) in <a href="/wiki/ESports" class="mw-redirect" title="ESports">esports</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-140" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-140">&#91;139&#93;</a></sup> 5 players, all of Faroese nationality, faced <a href="/wiki/Slovenia" title="Slovenia">Slovenia</a> in the first round, eventually getting knocked out with a 0–2 score.<sup id="cite_ref-141" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-141">&#91;140&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>At the 2016 Baku <a href="/wiki/Chess_Olympiad" title="Chess Olympiad">Chess Olympiad</a>, the Faroe Islands got their first chess grandmaster. <a href="/wiki/Helgi_Ziska" class="mw-redirect" title="Helgi Ziska">Helgi Ziska</a> won his third GM norm, and thus won the title of chess grandmaster.<sup id="cite_ref-142" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-142">&#91;141&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The Faroe Islands was given another chance to compete internationally in <a href="/wiki/ESports" class="mw-redirect" title="ESports">esports</a>, this time at the 2018 Northern European Minor Championship. The team captain was Rókur Dam Norðoy.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (April 2021)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Clothing">Clothing</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Faroe_Islands&amp;action=edit&amp;section=26" title="Edit section: Clothing">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Not to be confused with <a href="/wiki/Fair_Isle_(technique)" title="Fair Isle (technique)">Fair Isle (technique)</a>.</div>
<p>Faroese handicrafts are mainly based on materials available to local villages—mainly wool. Garments include sweaters, scarves, and gloves. Faroese jumpers have distinct Nordic patterns; each village has some regional variations handed down from mother to daughter. There has recently been a strong revival of interest in Faroese knitting, with young people knitting and wearing updated versions of old patterns emphasized by strong colours and bold patterns. This appears to be a reaction to the loss of traditional lifestyles, and as a way to maintain and assert cultural tradition in a rapidly-changing society. Many young people study and move abroad, and this helps them maintain cultural links with their specific Faroese heritage.
</p><p>There has also been a great interest in Faroese <a href="/wiki/Sweater" title="Sweater">sweaters</a><sup id="cite_ref-143" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-143">&#91;142&#93;</a></sup> from the TV series <i><a href="/wiki/The_Killing_(Danish_TV_series)" title="The Killing (Danish TV series)">The Killing</a></i>, where the main actress (Detective Inspector Sarah Lund, played by <a href="/wiki/Sofie_Gr%C3%A5b%C3%B8l" title="Sofie Gråbøl">Sofie Gråbøl</a>) wears Faroese sweaters.<sup id="cite_ref-144" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-144">&#91;143&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Lace_knitting" title="Lace knitting">Lace knitting</a> is a traditional handicraft. The most distinctive trait of <a href="/wiki/Faroese_lace_shawls" class="mw-redirect" title="Faroese lace shawls">Faroese lace shawls</a> is the centre-back <a href="/wiki/Gusset" title="Gusset">gusset</a> shaping. Each shawl consists of two triangular side panels, a trapezoid-shaped back gusset, an edge treatment, and usually shoulder shaping. These are worn by all generations of women, particularly as part of the traditional Faroese costume as an overgarment.
</p>
<div class="thumb tleft"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Faroese_folk_dance_club_from_vagar.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/62/Faroese_folk_dance_club_from_vagar.jpg/220px-Faroese_folk_dance_club_from_vagar.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="146" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/62/Faroese_folk_dance_club_from_vagar.jpg/330px-Faroese_folk_dance_club_from_vagar.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/62/Faroese_folk_dance_club_from_vagar.jpg/440px-Faroese_folk_dance_club_from_vagar.jpg 2x" data-file-width="640" data-file-height="426" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Faroese_folk_dance_club_from_vagar.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Faroese folk dancers, some of them in national costume</div></div></div>
<p>The traditional Faroese national dress is also a local handicraft that people spend a lot of time, money, and effort to assemble. It is worn at weddings and traditional dancing events, and on feast days. The cultural significance of the garment should not be underestimated, both as an expression of local and national identity and a passing on and reinforcing of traditional skills that bind local communities together.
</p><p>A young Faroese person is normally handed down a set of children's Faroese clothes that have passed from generation to generation. Children are <a href="/wiki/Confirmed" class="mw-redirect" title="Confirmed">confirmed</a> at age 14, and normally start to collect the pieces to make an adult outfit, which is considered as a rite of passage. Traditionally the aim would have been to complete the outfit by the time a young person was ready to marry and wear the clothes at the ceremony—though it is mainly only men who do this now.
</p><p>Each piece is intricately hand-knitted, dyed, woven or embroidered to the specifications of the wearer. For example, the man's waistcoat is put together by hand in bright blue, red or black fine wool. The front is then intricately embroidered with colourful silk threads, often by a female relative. The motifs are often local Faroese flowers or herbs. After this, a row of Faroese-made solid silver buttons are sewn on the outfit.
</p><p>Women wear embroidered silk, cotton or wool shawls and pinafores that can take months to weave or embroider with local flora and fauna. They are also adorned with a handwoven black and red ankle-length skirt, knitted black and red jumper, a velvet belt, and black 18th century style shoes with silver buckles. The outfit is held together by a row of solid silver buttons, silver chains and locally-made silver brooches and belt buckles, often fashioned with Viking style motifs.
</p><p>Both men's and women's national dress are extremely costly and can take many years to assemble. Women in the family often work together to assemble the outfits, including knitting the close-fitting jumpers, weaving and embroidering, sewing and assembling the national dress.
